⚠️ *The figure is a stratigraphic ordering with locational notes. Thickness and age figures are the source's own; the column is not drawn to any scale and the inset is a key to the source's maps, not a map.*

### 1. The question this lesson answers

⚠️ Arc A established that karst requires a **soluble rock with a tight matrix and abundant fractures**. Arc B must begin by asking: **which Indian rocks are that?** ⚠️ Without that answer, India's caves are a list of place names; with it, they are the predictable outcome of a stratigraphic distribution.

⚠️ The answer has two halves that behave quite differently. ⚠️ The **peninsular** half is Proterozoic — the Cuddapah and Vindhyan systems, with cement-grade limestone also in the Dharwars — and it is old, extensive, structurally deformed and economically dominant. ⚠️ The **north-eastern** half is **Palaeogene**, far younger, and it hosts essentially all of India's famous caves — and ❗ **it is missing from the spine book altogether**, which is the subject of L11.

### 2. The peninsular hosts, from the source

✅ **The Dharwars.** Khullar, printed folio 42 (local scan PDF p.38): *"The **Dharwars** are economically the most important rocks because they possess valuable minerals like high grade iron-ore, manganese, copper, lead, gold, quartzites, slates, mica, etc… jasper, asbestos, steatite and cherts. They also contain large deposits of building purpose quartzites and **cement grade limestones**."* ⚠️ The Dharwar carbonates are metamorphosed and discontinuous; they matter chiefly as **feedstock**, and little developed karst is attributed to them in any source available here.

✅ **The Purana frame.** *"**THE PURANA ROCK SYSTEM (1400–600 MILLION YEARS).** In India, the word purana has been used in place of **Proterozoic** and includes two divisions: the **Cuddapah System** and the **Vindhyas**."*

✅ **The Cuddapah System.** *"…a great thickness of **unfossilliferous clay, slates, quartzites, sandstones and limestones** was deposited presumably in great **synclinal basins**… The most extensive occurrence of this system is in **Cuddapah district followed by Kurnool district of Andhra Pradesh**. The outcrop is of an **irregular crescent shape concave towards the coast** covering an area of about **45,000 sq km**… Another large development of this system is in the southern parts of Chhattisgarh covering the districts of **Dantewara, Bastar, Kanker, Dhamtari, Raipur, Durg and Rajnandgaon**. A few isolated exposures occur in **Singhbhum district of Jharkhand, Kalahandi and Keonjhar districts of Odisha** and along the main axis of the **Aravalli range from Delhi to Idar in Gujarat** aggregating **5,200 metres** in thickness at certain places… In some parts of southern India, we have the Cuddapah system amounting to **6,100 metres** in thickness with several unconformities."*

✅ **The Vindhyan System.** *"**The Vindhyan System (1300–600 million years).** …a **vast stratified formation of sandstones, shales and limestones, often over 4000 m thick**… Occupying a large area of **over 1,00,000 sq km**, the Vindhyan system stretches from **Sasaram and Rohtas in western Bihar to Chittaurgarh in Rajasthan** with the exception of a **central tract in Bundelkhand which makes a gap in this belt**… **The Lower Vindhyan (1,300–1,100 million years) is marine in origin, mostly calcareous in nature** and shows tectonic deformation by folding movements… These rocks are also found in Chhattisgarh, **Bhima Valley of Karnataka and Kurnool district of Andhra Pradesh**… The Upper Vindhyan beds enclose two **diamond bearing horizons**, from which **Panna and Golconda** diamonds have been mined… provides large quantities of excellent and durable stones, flagstones, ornamental stones; **limestone**, pure glass making sand and some coal."*

❗ **The single most important clause for this topic is *"the Lower Vindhyan… mostly calcareous in nature"*** — because it identifies a **thick, laterally extensive, marine, folded carbonate** at a known stratigraphic position, which is precisely the host rock Arc A's conditions describe. 🔁 **Indian stratigraphy as a system belongs to the crust-and-rocks topic**; this edition borrows these rows **only as host rock**, and does not re-teach the systems.

✅ **The two peninsular terrains in which karst actually occurs.** Khullar, printed folios 82–83 (local scan PDF p.58), gives both:

| Terrain | ✅ Khullar's description | ⚠️ Karst significance |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Baghelkhand** | *"East of the Maikal Range… made of **limestones and sandstones** on the west and granite in the east… about **1.4 lakh sq km**… bounded by the **Son river** on the north, and to its south occur **anticlinal highlands and synclinal valleys of sandstones and limestones**… general elevation of **150 m to 1,200 m**… **scarps of the Vindhyan sandstones** between the Ganga plain and the Narmada-Son trough"* | ⚠️ The **structural** case (L3): interbedded carbonate, folded, with over a kilometre of relief and a clear outlet |
| ✅ **The Chhattisgarh Plain** | *"The whole basin lies between the Maikala Range and the Odisha hills… the basin is laid with **nearly horizontal beds of limestone and shales deposited during the Cuddapah age**. The general elevation… **250 m in the east to 330 m in the west**"* | ⚠️ The **bedding** case (L5): near-horizontal carbonate. 🇮🇳 **This is the host setting of the Bastar/Indravati cave country taught at L15** |

### 3. The extra-peninsular carbonates, and a superseded sentence

✅ Khullar, printed folio 45 (local scan PDF p.39), records carbonate at four further levels: Cambrian *"slates, clays, quartzites and **limestones**"*; Ordovician *"quartzites, grits, sandstones and **limestones**"*; Silurian and Devonian carbonates; and *"**The Carboniferous rocks (350 million years)** comprise mainly of **limestone**, shale and quartzite… **Mount Everest is composed of Upper Carboniferous limestone and dolomite**."*

⚠️ These are the **Himalayan and trans-Himalayan carbonate belts** — Spiti, Kashmir, Kumaon, the Pir Panjal — and they are genuine, thick, marine carbonates. ⚠️ Their karst potential is real but is **not documented in any source available to this edition**, and ❗ **no Himalayan karst landform or cave is claimed anywhere in this file.**

❌ **[N13] Correction — the Everest sentence is superseded.** ⚠️ The summit carbonate of Everest — the Qomolangma Formation — is dated **Ordovician**, not Upper Carboniferous. ⚠️ The correction is **minor to this topic**, because nothing in the karst argument depends on it, but it sits inside a highly quotable sentence and ❗ **a candidate who reproduces it is reproducing a checkable error**. ⚠️ ✅ Khullar's **general** statement about Carboniferous limestone, shale and quartzite is unaffected and may be used.

### 4. ❌ [N15] The arithmetic correction — a worked example of checking a quoted statistic

✅ Khullar, printed folio 825 (local scan PDF p.431): *"The total resources of limestone of all categories and grades are estimated at **84,935 million tonnes** of which **14,926 million tonnes (8 per cent) are under reserves category** and **170,009 million tonnes (92 per cent) are under remaining resources category**."*

```text
   THE CHECK, WHICH TAKES FIVE SECONDS AND SAVES A MARK

   STATED TOTAL ................................  84,935 Mt
   STATED COMPONENTS ...........  14,926 + 170,009 = 184,935 Mt
                                                    ^^^^^^^
   DISCREPANCY = exactly 100,000 Mt
   => the stated total is MISSING ITS LEADING DIGIT "1"

   NOW TEST WITH THE BOOK'S OWN PERCENTAGES:
      14,926 / 184,935 =  8.07 %   -> the book says  8 %   MATCHES
     170,009 / 184,935 = 91.93 %   -> the book says 92 %   MATCHES
      14,926 /  84,935 = 17.6  %   -> the book says  8 %   FAILS
     170,009 /  84,935 = 200   %   -> IMPOSSIBLE

   CONCLUSION: the intended total is 184,935 Mt.  The components and
   the percentages are INTERNALLY CONSISTENT; only the total is wrong.
   => CORRECT IT OPENLY, state the arithmetic, and move on.
```

❗ **Why this is taught as a method and not merely as a fix.** ⚠️ The canonical owner's chronology and data control requires that quantities be handled with their definitions and sources attached. ⚠️ The arithmetic test above — **do the parts sum to the stated whole, and do the stated percentages agree?** — is a thirty-second discipline that catches typographical corruption in any quoted statistic. ❗ **A candidate who writes *"84,935 million tonnes"* has copied a figure that cannot be true**, and the error is detectable without any external source.

### 5. ❌ [N16] Four incompatible rankings — and why the right response is to teach the distinction

| Source | Measure | Ranking as printed |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ **Khullar, folio 825** | **Resources**, share of total | *"**Karnataka is the leading state having 28 per cent** of the total resources"*, then **Andhra Pradesh 20 %, Rajasthan 12 %, Gujarat 11 %, Madhya Pradesh 12 %, Meghalaya 9 %, Chhattisgarh 5 %"* |
| ✅ **Khullar, folio 826** | **Cement-grade reserves** | **Andhra Pradesh** holds *"about **one-third** of the total reserves of the cement grade limestone"* |
| ✅ **Khullar, Table 23.13**, folio 826 (2011-12; *Mineral Wealth of India, 2013*) | **Annual production** | AP incl. Telangana **53,882 thousand t / 20.99 %**; Rajasthan **47,990 / 18.67**; MP **32,658 / 12.72**; Gujarat **24,224 / 9.44**; Tamil Nadu **21,736 / 8.47**; Karnataka **20,228 / 7.88**; Chhattisgarh **20,124 / 7.84**; Others **35,887 / 13.99**; Total **2,56,669 / 100.00** |
| ✅ **Majid, Table 10.43** (local scan PDF p.399; *Statistical Abstracts, 2007-08*) | **Annual production**, a different year | Rajasthan **240 th t / 16.15 %**; MP **238 / 16.01**; AP **235 / 15.81**; Gujarat **160 / 10.77**; Chhattisgarh **140 / 09.42**; Tamil Nadu **138 / 09.29**; Karnataka **125 / 08.41** |

❗ **These are not four errors. They are four different questions.**

```text
   RESOURCES          = everything identified, at ANY grade or confidence
   RESERVES           = the sub-set that is economically extractable NOW
   CEMENT-GRADE       = a FURTHER sub-set, filtered by CHEMISTRY
     RESERVES           (CaO high enough, impurities low enough)
   ANNUAL PRODUCTION  = what was actually QUARRIED in one named year

   A state can LEAD on one and TRAIL on another, legitimately:
     Karnataka leads on RESOURCES (28 %) but is SIXTH on 2011-12
       PRODUCTION (7.88 %)
     Andhra Pradesh leads on 2011-12 PRODUCTION (20.99 %) AND on
       CEMENT-GRADE RESERVES ("about one-third")
     MEGHALAYA holds 9 % of RESOURCES but DOES NOT APPEAR AS A NAMED
       ROW in the production table at all

   >>> THE ASYMMETRY IS THE ANSWER-WORTHY FINDING, NOT A PROBLEM <<<
```

❗ **The Meghalaya asymmetry is the single most valuable row in this table for Arc B.** ⚠️ **Nine per cent of national limestone resources, and not a named row in the production table** — that gap is the quantitative statement of a region whose carbonate is **abundant, high quality and comparatively under-exploited at the time of the data**, which is exactly the condition under which quarrying pressure subsequently grows. ❗ It is the empirical bridge from this lesson to the conflict at L15.

❗ **The discipline, stated as a rule.** ❗ **Never present a limestone league table.** ⚠️ Write instead: *"India's limestone statistics must be read against their measure and their year: on book-era data one state leads on total resources, another on cement-grade reserves, and a third on annual production, and Meghalaya holds a substantial resource share while not appearing among the named producing states."* ⚠️ That sentence is worth more than any ranking, because it demonstrates the data control the canonical owner requires.

### 6. The production series and the end-use split, with their date stamps

✅ **Khullar, folio 826:** *"Rapid pace of industrialisation in the country has resulted in an accelerated rate of production of limestone. It has increased from a mere **30.2 million tonnes in 1980-81** to over **256 million tonnes in 2011-12**."*

| ✅ **Table 23.12 — Production of Limestone in India (million tonnes)** | |
|---|---:|
| 1980-81 | **30.2** |
| 2008-09 | **222.0** |
| 2009-10 | **233.0** |
| 2010-11 | **238.0** |
| 2011-12 | **256.6** |

✅ Source line as printed: *"Statistical Outline of India (Tata) 2012-13, p. 67."*

✅ **End use** (folio 826): **75 per cent cement**, **16 per cent iron and steel**, **4 per cent chemicals**, the remainder in paper, sugar, fertilisers, glass, rubber and ferromanganese.

✅ **Dolomite** (folio 827): *"Limestone with more than **10 per cent** of magnesium is called dolomite; when the percentage rises to **45**, it is **true dolomite**."* Total resources **7,730 Mt**, of which **738 Mt** reserves and **6,992 Mt** remaining. 🔁 **The dolomite state tables are mineral geography and belong to the mineral-resources topic**; only the **10 per cent / 45 per cent definition** crosses over into this file, because it is a **rock-classification** fact that bears on solubility (L1, L11).

❗ **The date-stamp discipline, applied to all of the above.** ❗ **Every one of these figures is book-era.** ⚠️ The series ends at **2011-12**; the cited authorities are dated **2012-13** and **2013**; Majid's competing table cites **2007-08**. ❗ **No current limestone resource or production figure was fetched for this edition, and none is substituted.** ❗ Each figure must be written with its year and its source line, and the **structural reading** — that production roughly multiplied eightfold between the early 1980s and 2011-12, and that cement dominates end use — is what the argument uses, not the precise values.

### 7. 🇮🇳 India example and its limitation — what the host-rock spine does and does not establish

⚠️ **What it establishes.** India possesses, on the source's own evidence, **three structurally distinct carbonate provinces**: the **Proterozoic peninsular** systems (Cuddapah, Vindhyan, with Dharwar cement-grade limestone), the **Palaeozoic to Mesozoic extra-peninsular** belts, and the **Palaeogene north-eastern** carbonate of the Meghalaya plateau. ⚠️ The second is undocumented for karst; the first hosts India's peninsular cave country; **the third hosts essentially all of its famous caves**.

❗ **The limitation, stated rather than skipped, and it is the structural fact that organises the rest of Arc B.** ❗ **The spine book that supplies this entire host-rock column does not mention a single Meghalaya cave, and does not identify the Meghalaya plateau as carbonate at all.** ❗ Its Meghalaya entry gives the plateau as **Archaean basement** (corrected at **L11** as **[N6]**); ❗ its limestone occurrence list names *"**Meghalaya (Garo, Khasi and Jaintia hills)**"* without naming a formation; ❗ and its production table does not list Meghalaya as a named row. ⚠️ ❗ **Therefore the stratigraphic spine of India's most important karst region cannot be built from any book available to this edition, and is built at L11 from an official state-government source.** ⚠️ That is an unusual situation and it is declared rather than disguised.

### 8. Comparison, objection and the residual

| | Peninsular (Proterozoic) hosts | Extra-peninsular hosts | North-eastern (Palaeogene) host |
|---|---|---|---|
| ⚠️ Age | **1400–600 Ma** | Cambrian to Carboniferous | **Eocene** |
| ⚠️ Structure | **Folded**, with synclinal basins and unconformities | Strongly deformed, thrust | **Gently dipping**, fault-bounded on the south |
| ⚠️ Carbonate character | Interbedded with clastics; Lower Vindhyan *"mostly calcareous"* | Interbedded limestone, shale, quartzite | **Thick, high-purity, bedded members** |
| ⚠️ Documented karst | **Yes** — Bastar / Indravati, and the Baghelkhand structural setting | ❗ **None available to this edition** | **Yes — India's most extensive** |
| ⚠️ Economic role | **Dominant** in national production | Local | **Large resource share, small named production share** |
| ❗ Source coverage | Good, in the spine book | Thin | ❗ **Absent from the books; official source only** |

**Strongest objection.** ⚠️ This is Indian stratigraphy, which another topic owns. A karst lesson that spends half its length on the Cuddapah and Vindhyan systems has duplicated that owner.

**Reply.** ⚠️ The test applied throughout is **why the row is here**. ⚠️ Each system is admitted **only for its carbonate content and its structural disposition** — the two properties that determine whether solution can produce a landscape. ❗ What is **excluded** is everything the stratigraphic owner teaches: the rock cycle, the unconformity sequence as a sequence, the Gondwana coal measures, the Deccan traps, the diamond horizons beyond a single naming, the economic minerals of the Dharwars beyond the limestone clause, and the tectonic history. 🔁 All of it routes to the crust-and-rocks topic by name. ⚠️ Without the carbonate column, Arc B would be a list of cave names with no explanation of why those places and not others, which is precisely the gazetteer failure the roadmap rejected.

❓ **Residual, left open.** ⚠️ **Whether the extra-peninsular carbonate belts carry developed karst is not established by anything available to this edition.** ❗ The rock is attested; the landscape is not. ❗ **No Himalayan cave or karst landform is named anywhere in this file.** ❓ And **whether the Baghelkhand carbonates carry karst landforms is equally unestablished** — the structural description is in the source, the landform description is not.

### UPSC application

- **Syllabus:** ⚠️ *"Salient features of world's physical geography"* applied to India, General Studies Paper I, and the **distribution-of-natural-resources family** for the limestone data. 🔁 **Boundaries named:** Indian stratigraphy as a system → the crust-and-rocks topic; limestone and dolomite as a resource geography → the mineral-resources topic; the dolomite state tables → the same.
- **Verified PYQ:** ❗ **NONE OWNED (2018-2026).** ⚠️ The transferable asset is the **data-audit method**: checking that parts sum to a stated whole, and separating resources from reserves from grade-filtered reserves from annual production. ⚠️ That method is reusable across every resource question in the subject.
- **Traps:** ⚠️ Quoting **84,935** million tonnes. Presenting a limestone league table without saying what is being ranked. Treating a resource share as a production share. Quoting the 2011-12 series as current. Reproducing the Everest Carboniferous sentence. Assuming the Vindhyan is entirely sandstone when ✅ the **Lower Vindhyan is *"mostly calcareous"***. Assuming India's cave rock is peninsular.
- **Answer use:** ❗ When a question invites Indian limestone geography, lead with the **column and the measure discipline**, not the list: ⚠️ *"India's carbonate hosts fall into three provinces — Proterozoic Cuddapah and Vindhyan rocks in the peninsula, Palaeozoic belts in the extra-peninsular ranges, and Palaeogene limestone in Meghalaya — and India's limestone statistics must be read against whether they measure resources, reserves, cement-grade reserves or annual production, because the leading state differs under each."*

Revision notes:

- ⚠️ **Three carbonate provinces:** **Proterozoic peninsular** (Cuddapah, Vindhyan, Dharwar) · **Palaeozoic extra-peninsular** (Cambrian to Carboniferous) · **Palaeogene north-eastern** (Meghalaya, **L11**).
- ✅ **Dharwars:** *"large deposits of building purpose quartzites and **cement grade limestones**."*
- ✅ **Purana = Proterozoic**, 1400–600 Ma, comprising **Cuddapah** and **Vindhyan**.
- ✅ **Cuddapah:** *"clay, slates, quartzites, sandstones and **limestones**"* in *"great **synclinal** basins"*; **crescent concave to the coast**, ~**45,000 sq km**; up to **6,100 m** thick; S Chhattisgarh incl. **Bastar**; Aravalli axis **Delhi to Idar** (**5,200 m**); **Fig. 2.4**.
- ✅ **Vindhyan:** *"sandstones, shales and **limestones**, often over **4000 m** thick"*; >**1,00,000 sq km**; **Sasaram–Rohtas to Chittaurgarh** with a **Bundelkhand gap**; ✅ **Lower Vindhyan (1300–1100 Ma) *"marine in origin, mostly calcareous"***; **Bhima valley**, **Kurnool**; **Fig. 2.5**.
- ✅ **Baghelkhand** (folio 82): limestones and sandstones; *"**anticlinal highlands and synclinal valleys**"*; **1.4 lakh sq km**; **Son**; **150–1,200 m**.
- ✅ **Chhattisgarh** (folio 83): *"nearly **horizontal** beds of limestone and shales… **Cuddapah age**"*; **250–330 m**. 🇮🇳 **The Bastar cave host setting.**
- ❌ **[N13]:** ✅ *"Mount Everest is composed of **Upper Carboniferous** limestone and dolomite"* → ⚠️ the summit carbonate is **Ordovician**. ❗ Minor here, but checkable.
- ❌ **[N15]:** ✅ *"**84,935** million tonnes… of which **14,926** (8 %)… and **170,009** (92 %)"* → ⚠️ **14,926 + 170,009 = 184,935 Mt**, and the percentages confirm it. ❗ **The total is missing its leading digit.**
- ❌ **[N16]:** **four incompatible rankings** across two books and two facing pages — because they measure **resources / cement-grade reserves / production (2011-12) / production (2007-08)**. ❗ **Teach the distinction, never a league table.**
- ❗ **The Meghalaya asymmetry:** ✅ **9 % of national resources**, ❗ **not a named row in the production table**. ❗ The empirical bridge to **L15**.
- ✅ **Production series:** **30.2 Mt (1980-81) → 256.6 Mt (2011-12)**; source *"Statistical Outline of India (Tata) 2012-13, p. 67."* ❗ **Book-era; no current figure substituted.**
- ✅ **End use:** **75 % cement**, 16 % iron and steel, 4 % chemicals. ✅ **Dolomite definition:** >**10 %** magnesium = dolomite; >**45 %** = **true dolomite**. 🔁 The dolomite state tables go to the mineral-resources owner.
- ❗ **The books cannot build Meghalaya's cave stratigraphy.** ❗ It is built at **L11** from an **official state-government dossier**.
- 🔑 **Mnemonic for the peninsular hosts: D-C-V** — **D**harwar (cement grade), **C**uddapah (synclinal basins), **V**indhyan (**L**ower = calcareous). ❗ And the negative: **no limestone in the Gondwana.**

### Concept check

**Question:** A candidate writes: "India has 84,935 million tonnes of limestone resources, of which Karnataka leads with 28 per cent, and Andhra Pradesh is the largest producer with about one-third." Audit every quantitative claim in that sentence.

**Model answer:** Three claims are present and all three need correction or qualification. **First, the total is arithmetically impossible as stated.** The source gives the components as 14,926 million tonnes of reserves at 8 per cent and 170,009 million tonnes of remaining resources at 92 per cent. Those components sum to **184,935 million tonnes**, exactly 100,000 more than the stated total, so the printed total is missing its leading digit. The source's own percentages confirm the correction: 14,926 of 184,935 is 8.07 per cent and 170,009 of 184,935 is 91.93 per cent, matching the printed 8 and 92; whereas against the stated 84,935 the second component alone would exceed the total. The correct total is **184,935 million tonnes**, and the error is detectable without any external source. **Second, the Karnataka claim is correct but incompletely specified.** The 28 per cent is a share of **total resources of all categories and grades**, which is a different quantity from reserves, from cement-grade reserves and from annual production. Stated without that qualifier it invites the inference that Karnataka is India's leading limestone state in general, which the same book contradicts: on its 2011-12 production table Karnataka ranks sixth at 7.88 per cent. **Third, the Andhra Pradesh claim conflates two separate statements.** The same book says on one folio that Andhra Pradesh holds about one-third of the total reserves of **cement-grade** limestone, and on the facing folio that Andhra Pradesh including Telangana produced 20.99 per cent of national output in 2011-12. "About one-third" belongs to the grade-filtered reserve figure, not to production. **A fourth defect is the absence of dates.** Every figure cited is book-era: the production series ends at 2011-12 and the cited authorities are dated 2012-13 and 2013, while a second book's competing table cites 2007-08. The corrected sentence must therefore name the measure and the year for each figure, and should present the **asymmetry between resource share and production share** — most strikingly for Meghalaya, which holds 9 per cent of resources but appears as no named row in the production table — rather than any single ranking.

**Misconception to avoid:** Do not treat resources, reserves, grade-filtered reserves and annual production as interchangeable ways of saying "how much limestone a state has". They answer four different questions, and a state can legitimately lead on one while trailing on another; a ranking quoted without its measure is not evidence of anything.

**Original Mains practice [10 marks]:** "India's carbonate geology explains where its karst is, and where it is not." Examine. *(Examine — answer in about 150 words.)*

**Model (about 150 words):** India's carbonate rocks fall into three provinces with different karst outcomes. The Proterozoic peninsular systems supply the bulk: the Cuddapah system, deposited in great synclinal basins and cropping out as a crescent concave to the coast and again across southern Chhattisgarh, and the Vindhyan system, over a hundred thousand square kilometres thick in sandstone, shale and limestone, with its lower division marine and mostly calcareous. Where those beds lie nearly horizontal, as in the Chhattisgarh basin, cave country develops. The extra-peninsular belts carry Cambrian to Carboniferous limestones, but no karst landscape is documented for them here. The north-eastern province, Palaeogene limestone on the Meghalaya plateau, hosts India's most extensive cave systems. The negative is equally instructive: limestone occurs across almost the whole Indian column except the Gondwana, so rock alone does not predict karst — structure, relief, outlet and sustained recharge must coincide with it.

**Rubric (quantified, 10 marks):** 3 for the three-province structure with an age and a structural character for each; 2 for the Lower Vindhyan calcareous clause and the Cuddapah synclinal-basin clause used as host-rock evidence rather than as stratigraphy; 2 for a documented Indian karst terrain tied to its specific host; 2 for the negative argument that carbonate distribution exceeds karst distribution, with the Gondwana exception named; 1 for an explicit statement that one province's karst status is undocumented here rather than asserted. Deduct 2 for quoting the uncorrected resource total, and 1 for any limestone league table without its measure and year.

⚠️ *The figure is a schematic succession, not a surveyed section. The thickness values shown are the borehole figures reported in the official dossier for one named deposit and are not regional thicknesses; no distance, gradient or elevation on the figure is to scale.*

### 1. The question, and why no book can answer it

⚠️ Meghalaya holds India's most extensive mapped cave systems. ⚠️ The obvious question — **what rock are they in?** — turns out to be unanswerable from any of the four local books, and that is the structural fact that organises this lesson.

| Source | ⚠️ What it says about Meghalaya | ❗ What it does not say |
|---|---|---|
| ✅ Khullar, printed folio 83 | The plateau is *"largely formed by **Archaean (Dharwarian) quartzites, shales and schists** with granite intrusions and some basic silts"* | ❗ **Does not mention limestone at all**, and **names no cave** |
| ✅ Khullar, printed folio 826 | Limestone occurs in *"**Meghalaya (Garo, Khasi and Jaintia hills)**"* | ❗ **Names no formation, no member and no age** |
| ✅ Khullar, Table 23.13 | — | ❗ **Meghalaya is not a named row** in the 2011-12 production table |
| ✅ Majid, printed p.12.28-12.29 | State profile: area **22,429 sq km**, capital **Shillong**, languages **Khasi and Garo**, bounded by **Assam** to the north and east and **Bangladesh** to the south and west; *"Tourist Centres: **Elephant Falls, Chirrapunjji, Mawsynram, Bada-Pani**"*; **Fig. 12.19 Meghalaya** | ❗ **Names no cave** among the tourist centres |
| ✅ Majid, Table 10.43 | — | ❗ **Meghalaya does not appear** in the limestone production table |
| ✅ C2, the Advanced owner | *"Meghalaya contains the country's most extensive mapped limestone-cave systems"*; *"very high rainfall over thick limestone"* | ❗ **Supplies no stratigraphy whatever** — no formation, no member, no age |

❗ **Therefore the host-rock spine below is built entirely from an official state-government source, and every claim in it names that source.**

### 2. ❌ [N6 / F5] The correction — what the caves are actually in

📰 ✅ **CA1, official — Government of Meghalaya, Directorate of Mineral Resources, *Technical Data on the Various Limestone Deposits of Meghalaya*.**

✅ **For Lumshnong, Jaintia Hills (deposit 7):** *"The limestone belongs to the **Shella formation of the Jaintia Group of Eocene Age**. All the three **Sylhet limestone members** viz. **Upper (Prang), Middle (Umlatdoh) and Lower (Lakadong)** are present in the area. The younger **Kopili formation** characterised by **alternating bands of Shale and Sandstone**, represents the youngest sediments."* ✅ With borehole thicknesses: *"**Prang Limestone 92.65, Umlatdoh limestone 39.65, Lakadong limestone 61.80**"*, over an area of *"**76.8 sq.km.**"*

✅ **For Cherrapunji, East Khasi Hills (deposit 1):** *"Location: In the **Mawmluh-Mawsmai Hills South of Lower Cherra**… Area: **1.40 sq. km.**… The deposit is of **composite nature made up of limestone in the upper part and dolomite in the lower part. There is a transitional zone of dolomitic limestone in between.**… In the **Mawmluh Hill** a maximum thickness of **25.5 m** of limestone occurs in the upper part followed by pure dolomite in lower part… **The limestone belongs to the lower Sylhet Member of Shella formation.**"*

✅ **For Siju-Artheka, West Garo Hills (deposit 14):** *"Limestone occurs on **both side of Simsang river**. Maximum thickness **90 m** (Average 60 m)… The limestone belongs to **Upper Sylhet (Siju) Limestone Member of the Shella formation of Jaintia Group**. The limestone **rests conformably on the coal bearing Sylhet Sandstone (Tura) member and is overlain by Kopili Formation**."*

✅ **For Borsora, West Khasi Hills (deposit 6):** *"The limestone bed in the area represents the **top Sylhet Limestone Member** of the Shella Formation, **overlying the coal bearing Sylhet Sandstone Member**."*

❗ **The correction, stated plainly.**

| ❌ What the spine book says | ✅ What the official state source establishes |
|---|---|
| The Meghalaya plateau is *"largely formed by **Archaean (Dharwarian) quartzites, shales and schists** with granite intrusions"* | ⚠️ **True of the basement, and irrelevant to the caves.** Every Meghalaya cave named in any source available to this edition is in the **Palaeogene Sylhet Limestone** — the **Lakadong, Umlatdoh and Prang Members of the Shella Formation, Jaintia Group, Eocene** — resting unconformably on that Precambrian basement |

❗ **Why the correction is a release condition and not a refinement.** ⚠️ A candidate who writes *"Meghalaya's caves formed in the Archaean rocks of the Shillong plateau"* has made a **category error of roughly two billion years** and has described caves in **non-carbonate metamorphic rock**, which the entire mechanism of Arc A forbids. ⚠️ The book is not wrong about the plateau; it is **silent about the cover**, and the cover is where the caves are.

### 3. Why the caves are where they are — five controls, each with its evidence
